A multi-agent fire environment with exact stressor ablations

A pure-JAX multi-agent environment in which collapse seeds fire, smoke attenuates perception and every stressor switches off exactly, with a pre-registered test as a worked example.

hidden commentOWNER-PENDING:V3Q7 also RC1-22 / RC2-18: whether a short form of the siting limitation (C01) enters the abstract, here or in the post-check lesson

As a worked example, a pre-registered test asks whether one policy trained on co-occurring stressors beats one trained on each in isolation, at matched compute and a held-out near-critical severity. Joint training raises survival by 0.96RES-2 percentage points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [+0.47, +1.44]RES-2), about a tenthX4-3 of an agent per episode, detectable only in the last fifthRES-12 of training. The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. The correction trains an isolated arm without the thirdDES-8 of episodes whose only stressor, communication denial, had no detectable effect in calibration, matching the joint arm's share of training with an active stressor. Keeping that third was our design error; we registered the correction, outside the pre-registered test, before any outcome contrast was computed. The completion difference excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66RES-1 points. Both differences are sign unstable over the final half of training.

1 Introduction

V2 MATERIAL: paper/v2/sections/01_introduction.md (sentences may be reused where still true; mark them in provenance). DEPENDENCIES: §6.1 holds the full siting limitation; §4.4 the inert label and its asymmetry; §6.2 the mismatch and the design error; §6.3 the result; §6.5 the lesson.

When a training distribution has to cover several stressors, its designer chooses whether the policy meets them together or one at a time. Ecology studies the interactive and cumulative effects of multiple stressors [1, 2], and climate science the risk from compound events [3, 4]. In reinforcement learning the choice is a question of zero-shot generalization [5]: must a policy see stressors co-occur in training to cope with their co-occurrence at test time? Gao et al. (2024) [6] studied it for imitation-learned manipulation.

The Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), written in pure JAX, makes the question testable in multi-agent reinforcement learning (Figure 1). A cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode, at severities placed below, near and above a measured percolation threshold (§4.1). Two stressors are coupled to the fire: structural collapse can seed ignitions (Coupling A), and smoke attenuates each agent's observation by Beer–Lambert transmittance (Coupling B); a third, communication denial, is independent (§3.2). The fire is an ambient hazard: the reward never reads it and prices every death the same (§3.3). Switching a stressor off reproduces the environment without it bit for bit under the same random keys, and unit tests check the code against closed forms (§3.4). Section 5 describes how we measure the spread of identical reruns.

We use CHE for a worked example, a test registered before the main training runs. It compares a policy trained with every stressor on (the joint arm, JOINT) with one trained on each in isolation (the isolated arm, ISO), at matched compute, at a near-critical configuration neither arm trained on (§6.2). Joint training raises survival by 0.96RES-2 percentage points (Šidák-corrected 95 %DES-13 CI [+0.47, +1.44]RES-2), about a tenthX4-3 of an agent per episode (0.11X4-3 of 12ENV-6 agents), detectable only in the last fifthRES-12 of training (the checkpoints at updates 900 to 1000RES-12). Completion shows no detectable change, and the difference excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66RES-1 points. Both differences are sign unstable over the final half of training (§6.3). The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. A thirdDES-8 of the isolated arm's training episodes carried only communication denial; against an arm trained without them, joint training shows no detectable survival difference (§6.3). Keeping them was a design error, found before the main training runs; a correcting arm outside the pre-registered test was registered before unblinding, that is, before any outcome contrast was computed (§6.2).

The test was sited where composition is weak: at its severity, Coupling B's own survival effect in calibration was within a three-seedCB-9 spread, and communication denial had no detectable effect (§6.1). A post hoc interaction check at the test configuration measures whether the two couplings' effects on trained policies add (§6.4).HOLE:INTERACTIONfills (fix round, RC1-9, RM-8): extend the sentence before this marker in place with the check's pooled reading per PLAN §7, labelled post hoc, e.g. "… add: <reading> (§6.4)"; about 13 words of room are left under the 600-word budget Communication denial is labelled inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale; the label rests on coarse acceptance thresholds and is applied asymmetrically across stressors (§4.4). The isolated arm and its corrected version trained every episode that contained a coupling with links intact, while the test configuration denies every link, a train/test mismatch (§6.2). Every learned policy comes from one algorithm, shared-parameter independent PPO [7], on one 64 × 64ENV-6 map with 12ENV-6 agents; the worked example has one held-out test point and no heuristic baseline.

Our contributions are:

  1. CHE and its code (§3, §8).
  2. What calibration found: under policies trained for 500DX-40 updates, collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-foldCA-4 from low to high severity, and, descriptively, smoke lowers survival at high severity in 3 of 3CB-5 contrasts (§4).
  3. The worked example and its lesson: matching the share of training with an active stressor cannot by itself isolate co-occurrence (§6.5).

When a training distribution has to cover several stressors, its designer chooses whether the policy meets them together or one at a time. Ecology studies the interactive and cumulative effects of multiple stressors <d-cite key="crain2008stressors,cote2016stressors"></d-cite>, and climate science the risk from compound events <d-cite key="zscheischler2018compound,zscheischler2020typology"></d-cite>. In reinforcement learning the choice is a question of zero-shot generalization <d-cite key="kirk2023zsg"></d-cite>: must a policy see stressors co-occur in training to cope with their co-occurrence at test time? Gao et al. (2024) <d-cite key="gao2024composition"></d-cite> studied it for imitation-learned manipulation.

The Compound Hostile Environment (CHE), written in pure JAX, makes the question testable in multi-agent reinforcement learning (Figure 1). A cellular-automaton fire burns in every episode, at severities placed below, near and above a measured percolation threshold (§4.1). Two stressors are coupled to the fire: structural collapse can seed ignitions (Coupling A), and smoke attenuates each agent's observation by Beer–Lambert transmittance (Coupling B); a third, communication denial, is independent (§3.2). The fire is an ambient hazard: the reward never reads it and prices every death the same (§3.3). Switching a stressor off reproduces the environment without it bit for bit under the same random keys, and unit tests check the code against closed forms (§3.4). Section 5 describes how we measure the spread of identical reruns.

We use CHE for a worked example, a test registered before the main training runs. It compares a policy trained with every stressor on (the joint arm, JOINT) with one trained on each in isolation (the isolated arm, ISO), at matched compute, at a near-critical configuration neither arm trained on (§6.2). Joint training raises survival by 0.96<!-- N:RES-2 --> percentage points (Šidák-corrected 95 %<!-- N:DES-13 --> CI [+0.47, +1.44]<!-- N:RES-2 -->), about a tenth<!-- N:X4-3 --> of an agent per episode (0.11<!-- N:X4-3 --> of 12<!-- N:ENV-6 --> agents), detectable only in the last fifth<!-- N:RES-12 --> of training (the checkpoints at updates 900 to 1000<!-- N:RES-12 -->). Completion shows no detectable change, and the difference excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66<!-- N:RES-1 --> points. Both differences are sign unstable over the final half of training (§6.3). The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. A third<!-- N:DES-8 --> of the isolated arm's training episodes carried only communication denial; against an arm trained without them, joint training shows no detectable survival difference (§6.3). Keeping them was a design error, found before the main training runs; a correcting arm outside the pre-registered test was registered before unblinding, that is, before any outcome contrast was computed (§6.2).

The test was sited where composition is weak: at its severity, Coupling B's own survival effect in calibration was within a three-seed<!-- N:CB-9 --> spread, and communication denial had no detectable effect (§6.1). A post hoc interaction check at the test configuration measures whether the two couplings' effects on trained policies add (§6.4).<!-- HOLE:INTERACTION --><!-- fills (fix round, RC1-9, RM-8): extend the sentence before this marker in place with the check's pooled reading per PLAN §7, labelled post hoc, e.g. "… add: <reading> (§6.4)"; about 13 words of room are left under the 600-word budget --> Communication denial is labelled inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale; the label rests on coarse acceptance thresholds and is applied asymmetrically across stressors (§4.4). The isolated arm and its corrected version trained every episode that contained a coupling with links intact, while the test configuration denies every link, a train/test mismatch (§6.2). Every learned policy comes from one algorithm, shared-parameter independent PPO <d-cite key="dewitt2020ippo"></d-cite>, on one 64 × 64<!-- N:ENV-6 --> map with 12<!-- N:ENV-6 --> agents; the worked example has one held-out test point and no heuristic baseline.

Our contributions are:

1. CHE and its code (§3, §8).
2. What calibration found: under policies trained for 500<!-- N:DX-40 --> updates, collapse-seeded ignitions fall 4.8-fold<!-- N:CA-4 --> from low to high severity, and, descriptively, smoke lowers survival at high severity in 3 of 3<!-- N:CB-5 --> contrasts (§4).
3. The worked example and its lesson: matching the share of training with an active stressor cannot by itself isolate co-occurrence (§6.5).

Table 1. Where the hazard enters the learning or planning problem.

work agents and method where the hazard enters
Haksar & Schwager (2018) [8] aerial agents; multi-agent deep Q-network the task: a reward for moving to burning boundary trees; agents have no damage state
JaxWildfire (Çakır et al., 2025) [9] one air-tanker agent; JAX simulator the reward: a penalty proportional to the number of burning cells; agent harm not described
safe reinforcement learning (García & Fernández, 2015) [10] maximizing return under safety constraints a constraint or a cost, typically
VULCAN (Liu & Yan, 2026) [11] proposed fire search and rescue, planned by a vision-language model a hazard cost map of smoke, heat and sensing uncertainty
CHE (this paper) 12ENV-6 agents; independent PPO ambient: transition and observation kernels only; the reward prices each death at \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause

For Coupling A, the nearest neighbour we found is Raut et al. (2026) [12], a single-agent model of public-safety power shutoffs in which power flows resulting from the operator's switching set the failure probability of lines in wildfire areas, treated as ignition risk. OWNER-PENDING:v2C1 (carried v2 question C1 / Q4a: READS RULING item 2 recorded the owner's section 2 as final; this sentence keeps v2's accuracy fix OI-4 of the "about seven percent" sentence; DISPOSITIONS §5 gives C1 no V3Q number)In CHE, ignition is not an action available to the agents, though where they stand changes collapse rates (§3.2). For Coupling B, VULCAN degrades simulated sensing with smoke but has no transmittance model, and JaxWildfire's observations are partial but not attenuated. Our search found no reinforcement-learning environment whose observation kernel attenuates by transmittance. Among the JAX multi-agent benchmarks we surveyed, JaxMARL [13], Multi-Agent Craftax [14] and Assistax [15], none has an evolving, spreading lethal hazard field or perception attenuated by a hazard.

Domain randomization and unsupervised environment design ask which distribution to train on, the latter adaptively [16, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21]; our worked example compares fixed distributions on a held-out configuration, a zero-shot generalization test [5], and evaluates no adaptive curriculum.

The closest earlier study of our question is Gao et al. (2024) [6], who ask, in visual imitation learning for robotic manipulation, whether policies compose environmental factors to succeed on unseen combinations. OWNER-PENDING:v2C1 (as above: v2's accuracy fix RC1-24 of the owner's "the strategy that covers more combinations does better"; RWV Part 3)Comparing data-collection strategies, they find that policies do compose and that a strategy cycling through factors, so that more combinations appear, is generally the most effective; their design, built for data efficiency, does not separate co-occurrence from how often each factor value appears. The DBCA method [22] builds train and test splits with similar distributions of single elements and different distributions of their combinations; our secondary sweep follows that idea, holding each coupling's frequency fixed while co-occurrence varies (Appendix F). OWNER-PENDING:v2C1 (as above: v2's accuracy fixes OI-3 of the owner's Erdem and Üre sentences)In robust reinforcement learning, Erdem & Üre (2025) [23] report that single-agent policies trained against an adversary on one modality, observations or actions, remain vulnerable to simultaneous attacks on both (their Tables 3–4). Their attacked runs schedule no unattacked steps, and runs attacked on both modalities carry both budgets, so per-modality frequency, co-occurrence and total budget change together.

A survey of adaptability in multi-agent reinforcement learning (Hu et al., 2025, revised 2026) [24] calls an environment family highly diagnostic if it "systematically varies" factors such as observability or communication structure "while holding other factors fixed". It mentions compositional generalization but analyses no shift in several factors at once. Wherever we say that no work does something, we mean none found by the search of Appendix I.

Pattern: (?i)\bagent-independent\b EVERY OTHER CAVEAT in checks/caveats.yaml: pointer only ("§6.3", "Appendix D"), never restated. C26: any sentence about the interaction check carries "post hoc". NOT HERE: "finite Dec-POMDP" without "on its reachable states"; "one transition kernel" (it is "one transition or observation kernel"); Remark 1 (cut); "the formal specification"; JaxMARL compatibility; Thm. 1's v2 closed form (it does not describe the toy, BRIEF §9.2 trap 1); obs v2/v3, E2C, handshake, digest, traced (banned); PBT. V2 MATERIAL: paper/v2/sections/03_environment.md (most of 3.1 to 3.5), 05_coupling_a.md §5.1, 06_coupling_b.md §6.1, 07_comms.md §7.1 (definitions). DEPENDENCIES: Appendix A (W2) holds the proofs and Thm. 1's statement; §4 (W3) holds what each stressor does; §8 (W5) the API.

CHE is a grid world in which a team of agents forages while a fire burns (Figure 2).

3.1 State, step order, fire and smoke

Definition 1 (Compound Hostile Environment). A CHE is a Dec-POMDP \(\mathcal{M}_\theta = \langle I, \mathcal{S}, \{\mathcal{A}_i\}, T_\theta, R, \{\Omega_i\}, O_\theta, \gamma \rangle\) on a grid \(G\), indexed by the environment configuration \(\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)\) of Definitions 3, 5, 6 and 7. Its state \(s = (x, h, \rho, c, k, t)\) holds the agents \(x\) (positions, alive flags \(\alpha_i\), task state), the fire \(h \in \{\mathrm{Fuel}, \mathrm{Burning}, \mathrm{Burnt}\}^G\), the smoke \(\rho \in \mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}^G\), the structure \(c \in \{\mathrm{intact}, \mathrm{collapsed}\}^G\), the link graph \(k\) and the step \(t \le T\). The transition kernel factorizes in this order:

\[\begin{aligned} T_\theta(s' \mid s, a) = {} & T_C(c' \mid c, x)\; T_H^{\beta,\kappa_A}(h' \mid h, c, c')\; T_\rho(\rho' \mid \rho, h') \\ & \times T_X(x' \mid x, a, h', c')\; T_K^{\delta}(k' \mid x'), \end{aligned}\]

and agent \(i\) observes the post-step state, \(o'_i \sim O^i_{\kappa_B}(\cdot \mid x', h', \rho', c', k')\).

By Proposition 1 (Appendix A), \(\mathcal{M}_\theta\) is a well-defined Dec-POMDP whose states and observations reachable within the horizon form finite sets. A constant is locked if it was fixed before the main experiment (values in Appendix B, dates in Appendix D).

Definition 3 (Fire and smoke). Each cell is Fuel, Burning or Burnt. At each step, independently for each ordered pair of a Burning cell \(g\) and a Fuel von Neumann neighbour \(g'\), the cell \(g'\) ignites with probability \(\beta\); a Burning cell is Burnt one step later, and Burnt is absorbing. Spontaneous ignition is off (\(\iota = 0\)DX-1). Smoke is state, and it outlives flame: per cell,

\[\rho' = e^{-\eta}\rho + \sigma_s \mathbf{1}[h' = \mathrm{Burning}],\]

with \(\sigma_s = 1.0\) and \(\eta = 0.5\)DX-1 fixed by convention, never calibrated, and no spatial smoothing.

Every episode starts from an all-Fuel grid with oneV3-1 Burning cell at a uniformly random position. With Coupling A off, the cells that ever burn then form a bond-percolation cluster with edge probability \(\beta\) (Proposition 2, Appendix A)[25, 26], which §4.1 uses to place severity.

hidden commentFIGURE 2: block carried from paper/v2/sections/03_environment.md:25 (include and alt text as v2 had them); caption replaced

Collapse, fire, smoke, agents and communication are sampled in this order. Coupling A (orange) carries c′ − c into the fire; Coupling B (blue) carries the smoke ρ′ into the observation, drawn from the post-step state. Denial δ enters the communication kernel, which reads no hazard.

3.2 The stressors

Definition 5 (Coupling A, as implemented). Weak terrain \(w(g) \in \{0, 1\}\) marks a clustered fraction \(f_{\mathrm{weak}}\) of the cells, drawn per episode, and \(o_t(g) \in \{0, 1\}\) marks at least one alive agent on cell \(g\) before the step. An intact cell collapses with probability

\[\lambda_t(g) = \big(\lambda_0 + \lambda_{\mathrm{load}}\, o_t(g)\big)\, w(g),\]

and collapse is absorbing. Coupling A then ignites, with probability \(\kappa_A\), each cell still Fuel after this step's spread within Chebyshev distance \(r_A\) of a cell that collapsed in this step: the fire reads the increment \(c' - c\).

hidden commentLISTING 1: block carried byte-for-byte from paper/v2/sections/05_coupling_a.md:48; move it, never edit it

Listing 1. Coupling A's seeding mask: its uniforms are drawn before \(\kappa_A\) is read, and it acts on \(c' - c\) only.

hidden commentLISTING: che/env/structure.py:83-102
# che/env/structure.py:83-102 (verbatim)
def coupling_a_seed_mask(
    key: jax.Array,
    collapse_increment: jax.Array,
    *,
    kappa_A: float,
    r_seed: int,
) -> jax.Array:
    """Coupling A (Def. 5): each cell in the seeding neighborhood N_A of a
    new collapse (Chebyshev radius r_seed) ignites independently w.p.
    kappa_A. `collapse_increment` is the boolean mask of c' - c, i.e. cells
    that collapsed *this step* — T_H reads the increment, not the stock.
    Returns the candidate ignition mask; `hazard.seed_ignitions` applies it
    to Fuel cells only.
    """
    chex.assert_rank(collapse_increment, 2)
    chex.assert_type(collapse_increment, jnp.bool_)
    # Invariant #3: uniforms drawn even when kappa_A == 0.
    u = jax.random.uniform(key, collapse_increment.shape)
    near_new_collapse = dilate(collapse_increment, r_seed)
    return near_new_collapse & (u < kappa_A)

The hazard is not agent-independent: a weak cell with an agent on it collapses nineCA-8 times as often as an empty one. Below the percolation threshold, with collapses as the fire's only source and their clusters apart, the expected burnt area grows linearly in the collapse rate (Proposition 3, checked in twoDX-304 runs at parameters other than the locked ones; Appendix A).

Definition 6 (Coupling B, as implemented). For agent \(i\) at \(x_i\) and a cell \(y\) of its crop,

\[\tau_i(y) = \exp\!\big(-\kappa_B\, \lVert y - x_i \rVert_2\, \bar\rho_i(y)\big),\]

a midpoint-rule Beer–Lambert transmittance, where \(\bar\rho_i(y)\) averages \(\rho'\) at fourX2-7 evenly spaced points on the line of sight. Each crop cell is revealed independently with probability \(\tau_i(y)\); an unrevealed cell passes zeros, and a visibility plane records the reveal. Setting \(\kappa_B = 0\) gives \(\tau \equiv 1\) (Listing 4, Appendix A).

Because \(\rho'\) is hazard state, perception degrades where and when the fire is active.

In a corridor toy built on the same observation code, ignoring the attenuated signal costs exactly half the probability of being informed before committing, and this cost falls continuously to zero as \(\kappa_B\) grows (Theorem 1, Appendix A); this concerns optimal values in the toy, not learners in CHE.

Definition 7 (Communication denial, as implemented). After the agents move, each directed link between alive agents \(i \ne j\) opens with probability

\[\Pr\big[\, i \to j \text{ open} \,\big] = \mathbf{1}\big[\lVert x'_i - x'_j \rVert_\infty \le R_{\mathrm{comm}}\big]\,(1 - \delta),\]

with \(R_{\mathrm{comm}} = 16\)COM-6 cells. Messages \(m_i \in [-1, 1]^{8}\)DX-17 from a frozen, randomly initialized message head (Appendix B) arrive one step later as the mean over a receiver's open links.

\(T_K\) reads positions and aliveness only, so denial is independent of the fire.

3.3 A hazard-blind reward

Definition 2 (Ambient hazard). The hazard is ambient if (i) the reward reads task variables and the agents' alive transition only,

\[\begin{aligned} R(s,a,s') = {} & R_{\mathrm{task}}(\mathrm{task}(x), a, \mathrm{task}(x')) \\ & - d_p\,\lvert\{i : \alpha_i = 1,\ \alpha'_i = 0\}\rvert , \end{aligned}\]

with no term in \(h\), \(\rho\) or \(c\) and no auxiliary cost or constraint; and (ii) \(T_H\) is a fixed stochastic kernel that nothing in the environment optimizes.

In CHE the task variables are the food grid and the alive agents' positions, and each death costs \(d_p = 0.5\)ENV-1, whatever its cause.

hidden commentLISTING 2: block carried byte-for-byte from paper/v2/sections/03_environment.md:167 (Listing 3 in draft1; renumbered in the fix round); move it, never edit it

Listing 2. The task reward's signature admits the food and occupancy grids only.

hidden commentLISTING: che/env/tasks.py:31-43
# che/env/tasks.py:31-43 (verbatim)
def task_step(food: jax.Array, occupancy: jax.Array) -> tuple[jax.Array, jax.Array]:
    """Collection: an occupied food cell yields +1 team reward and empties.

    Multiple agents on one food cell still collect a single item (+1).
    Returns (food', team_reward). Reads task variables and agent occupancy
    only — no hazard/smoke/structure arguments, by design (Def. 2).
    """
    chex.assert_type(food, jnp.uint8)
    chex.assert_equal_shape([food, occupancy])
    collected = (food == 1) & occupancy
    reward = collected.sum().astype(jnp.float32)
    food_new = jnp.where(collected, jnp.uint8(0), food).astype(jnp.uint8)
    return food_new, reward

Two tests check that the reward reads no hazard directly: states that differ only in fire, smoke or structure, with the alive transition held equal, get equal rewards, and a death by fire or by collapse changes the reward by \(-d_p\).

An agent dies only on a cell that is burning or collapsing, so the cause-blind death price is in effect a hazard-contact price, and it drives survival learning. In the one ablation of \(d_p\), raising it from 0ENV-2 to 0.5ENV-1 raised survival at High severity (§4.1) from 0.575 to 0.866ENV-2 (Appendix B). Survival thus enters learning as a penalized channel and completion, at +1ENV-9 per item, as a rewarded one; any asymmetry between them is read that way.

3.4 Exact nesting, and theory checked as tests

Each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel: setting \(\kappa_A = 0\) gives a fire that ignores the structure, \(\kappa_B = 0\) the observation before masking plus an all-ones visibility plane, and \(\delta = 0\) free, range-limited communication.

These are nested models because every stochastic branch draws its uniforms unconditionally and compares them with a probability that may be zero. A zero therefore changes comparisons only, and the same keys reproduce the nested model's trajectory bit for bit on one backend; Listing 5 (Appendix A) is the test for \(\kappa_B = 0\).

The test for \(\kappa_A = 0\) compares fire and smoke only, since agents respond to collapses; the test for \(\delta = 0\) matches, on the CPU, a trajectory hash recorded before the communication channel existed.

Propositions 2 and 3, Corollary 1 and Theorem 1 are exact results that the code is tested against, in toys and at parameters other than the locked ones; the paper claims no theory beyond them.

Table 2. Properties of CHE with the listing (3–5 in Appendix A), test (che/tests/test_*.py, code supplement) and figure carrying each.

property listing test fig.
step order of Definition 1 (Prop. 1) 3 hazard, lethality 2
fire is bond percolation; severity by measured threshold (Prop. 2, Cor. 1) — percolation 3
collapse seeds fire (Coupling A, Prop. 3) 1 coupling_a, prop3 1, 4
smoke attenuates perception (Coupling B, Thm. 1) 4 coupling_b, e2c 1, 5
communication denial reads no hazard 3 comms —
hazard-blind reward, cause-blind death price (Def. 2) 2 reward_independence, lethality —
ablations nest bitwise 5 nesting, coupling_b, comms —
locked constants reachable from configurations — locks —
floors per metric, GPU and artifact (§5) — — C1

3.5 Instance, training and metrics

Every trained policy, and every evaluation of one, uses one instance: a 64 × 64 grid, 12 agents, a horizon of 256 steps, a 9 × 9 egocentric crop and 32 food itemsENV-6.

Each seed trains one shared-parameter actor-critic policyENV-11 (Appendix B) with the independent PPO of de Witt et al. (2020)[7], in the style of Lu et al. (2022)[27], at 32,768DX-3 environment steps per update.

Both outcomes are per episode: completion is \(1 - F/32\)ENV-6, with \(F\) the food left at the end, and survival is the fraction of the 12ENV-6 agents alive at the end. Episodes always run to the horizon.

A coupling-co-active counter logs the collapse-seeded ignitions within Chebyshev radius 4DX-37 of an alive agent. Only Coupling A produces them and \(\kappa_B\) does not enter the count, so it is co-active only in all-on configurations.

**Definition 1 (Compound Hostile Environment).** A CHE is a Dec-POMDP $\mathcal{M}_\theta = \langle I, \mathcal{S}, \{\mathcal{A}_i\}, T_\theta, R, \{\Omega_i\}, O_\theta, \gamma \rangle$ on a grid $G$, indexed by the *environment configuration* $\theta = (\beta, \kappa_A, \kappa_B, \delta)$ of Definitions 3, 5, 6 and 7.     *,

### 3.3 A hazard-blind reward

**Definition 2 (Ambient hazard).** The hazard is *ambient* if (i) the reward reads task variables and the agents' alive transition only,

$$
\begin{aligned}
R(s,a,s') = {} & R_{\mathrm{task}}(\mathrm{task}(x), a, \mathrm{task}(x')) \\

\end{aligned}
$$

with no term in $h$, $\rho$ or $c$ and no auxiliary cost or constraint; and (ii) $T_H$ is a fixed stochastic kernel that nothing in the environment optimizes.

In CHE the task variables are the food grid and the alive agents' positions, and each death costs $d_p = 0.5$<!-- N:ENV-1 -->, whatever its cause.

**Listing 2.** The task reward's signature admits the food and occupancy grids only.

```python
# che/env/tasks.py:31-43 (verbatim)
def task_step(food: jax.Array, occupancy: jax.Array) -> tuple[jax.Array, jax.Array]:
    """Collection: an occupied food cell yields +1 team reward and empties.

    Multiple agents on one food cell still collect a single item (+1).
    Returns (food', team_reward). Reads task variables and agent occupancy
    only — no hazard/smoke/structure arguments, by design (Def. 2).
    """
    chex.assert_type(food, jnp.uint8)
    chex.assert_equal_shape([food, occupancy])
    collected = (food == 1) & occupancy
    reward = collected.sum().astype(jnp.float32)
    food_new = jnp.where(collected, jnp.uint8(0), food).astype(jnp.uint8)
    return food_new, reward
```

Two tests check that the reward reads no hazard directly: states that differ only in fire, smoke or structure, with the alive transition held equal, get equal rewards, and a death by fire or by collapse changes the reward by $-d_p$.

An agent dies only on a cell that is burning or collapsing, so the cause-blind death price is in effect a hazard-contact price, and it drives survival learning. In the one ablation of $d_p$, raising it from 0<!-- N:ENV-2 --> to 0.5<!-- N:ENV-1 --> raised survival at High severity (§4.1) from 0.575 to 0.866<!-- N:ENV-2 --> (Appendix B). Survival thus enters learning as a penalized channel and completion, at +1<!-- N:ENV-9 --> per item, as a rewarded one; any asymmetry between them is read that way.

### 3.4 Exact nesting, and theory checked as tests

Each stressor is a parameter of one transition or observation kernel: setting $\kappa_A = 0$ gives a fire that ignores the structure, $\kappa_B = 0$ the observation before masking plus an all-ones visibility plane, and $\delta = 0$ free, range-limited communication.

These are nested models because every stochastic branch draws its uniforms unconditionally and compares them with a probability that may be zero. A zero therefore changes comparisons only, and the same keys reproduce the nested model's trajectory bit for bit on one backend; Listing 5 (Appendix A) is the test for $\kappa_B = 0$.

The test for $\kappa_A = 0$ compares fire and smoke only, since agents respond to collapses; the test for $\delta = 0$ matches, on the CPU, a trajectory hash recorded before the communication channel existed.

Propositions 2 and 3, Corollary 1 and Theorem 1 are exact results that the code is tested against, in toys and at parameters other than the locked ones; the paper claims no theory beyond them.

**Table 2.** Properties of CHE with the listing (3–5 in Appendix A), test (`che/tests/test_*.py`, code supplement) and figure carrying each.

| property | listing | test | fig. |
|---|---|---|---|
| step order of Definition 1 (Prop. 1) | 3 | `hazard`, `lethality` | 2 |
| fire is bond percolation; severity by measured threshold (Prop. 2, Cor. 1) | — | `percolation` | 3 |
| collapse seeds fire (Coupling A, Prop. 3) | 1 | `coupling_a`, `prop3` | 1, 4 |
| smoke attenuates perception (Coupling B, Thm. 1) | 4 | `coupling_b`, `e2c` | 1, 5 |
| communication denial reads no hazard | 3 | `comms` | — |
| hazard-blind reward, cause-blind death price (Def. 2) | 2 | `reward_independence`, `lethality` | — |
| ablations nest bitwise | 5 | `nesting`, `coupling_b`, `comms` | — |
| locked constants reachable from configurations | — | `locks` | — |
| floors per metric, GPU and artifact (§5) | — | — | C1 |

### 3.5 Instance, training and metrics

Every trained policy, and every evaluation of one, uses one instance: a 64 × 64 grid, 12 agents, a horizon of 256 steps, a 9 × 9 egocentric crop and 32 food items<!-- N:ENV-6 -->.

Each seed trains one shared-parameter actor-critic policy<!-- N:ENV-11 --> (Appendix B) with the independent PPO of de Witt et al. (2020)<d-cite key="dewitt2020ippo"></d-cite>, in the style of Lu et al. (2022)<d-cite key="lu2022dpo"></d-cite>, at 32,768<!-- N:DX-3 --> environment steps per update.

Both outcomes are per episode: *completion* is $1 - F/32$<!-- N:ENV-6 -->, with $F$ the food left at the end, and *survival* is the fraction of the 12<!-- N:ENV-6 --> agents alive at the end. Episodes always run to the horizon.

A coupling-co-active counter logs the collapse-seeded ignitions within Chebyshev radius 4<!-- N:DX-37 --> of an alive agent. Only Coupling A produces them and $\kappa_B$ does not enter the count, so it is co-active only in all-on configurations.

4.1 Severity, placed by a measured threshold

Definition 4 (Severity regimes). For an arena of side \(L\), let \(\xi(\beta)\) be the typical extent of a fire cluster.

  • Low, subcritical, \(\xi(\beta) \ll L\): fires stay local.
  • Medium, near-critical, \(\xi(\beta) \sim L\): one ignition can cascade to the scale of the arena.
  • High, supercritical: the fire front moves about as fast as the agents.

Operationally, each level is the observable band fixed for it before the main experiment (table). The calibration ran the fire alone on all-fuel grids (Figure 3), measuring the spanning probability: the chance that one centre ignition reaches the boundary. Three estimators shipped with the calibration data place the critical point between 0.480 and 0.513DX-204, with consensus mean 0.499DX-204; the value fixed for the experiment, 0.500 ± 0.005CAL-1, comes from logistic fits that no committed estimator reproduces. OWNER-PENDING:v2D2 (carried v2 question D2: Corollary 1(4) stated at the Garet and Marchand level; DISPOSITIONS §5 gives it no V3Q number)On the infinite square lattice, the idealized kernel's critical point is exactly ½ (Kesten, 1980)[26], and above it a fire that spreads indefinitely burns a region growing linearly towards a deterministic convex shape (Garet & Marchand, 2004[28]; Corollary 1, Appendix A). The agreement checks the implementation and says nothing new about fire.

severity \(\beta\) phase spanning probability (\(L = 64\)DX-7) burnt fraction band it was fixed to
Low 0.43CAL-3 subcritical 0.021CAL-3 1.9 %CAL-3 spanning below 0.05, burnt 1–5 %DX-201
Medium 0.49CAL-3 near-critical 0.547CAL-3 19.8 %CAL-3 spanning 0.3–0.7DX-201
High 0.70CAL-3 supercritical 0.998CAL-3 98.3 %CAL-3 front speed 0.5–1.0 cells per stepDX-201, measured 0.83DX-6

Medium, the test severity of §6, lies between the 64DX-7-cell arena's pseudo-critical point, about 0.486CAL-4, and the fixed critical point. Definition 4 also expected outcomes to vary most at Medium. Re-tested with fixed fire-only policies, that expectation is refuted for survival, whose per-episode variance rises with severity (0.00091, 0.00709, 0.00885CAL-5).

4.2 Coupling A

In the runs of the Coupling-B calibration grid with Coupling B on (500-updateDX-40 policies at each severity), collapse-seeded ignitions per episode fall from 4.05 at Low and 3.41 at Medium to 0.84 at HighCA-4. This 4.83-foldCA-4 fall (from unrounded counts) is fuel exhaustion (a seed lights only unburnt fuel), first seen under random policies at about 5.7-foldDX-9. The Coupling-A calibration grid (other policies, Coupling A off and on) replicates it at 4.92-foldCA-5. In all nineCA-6 measured cells, the share of seeded ignitions inside an alive agent's crop stays between 0.164 and 0.203CA-6 (Figure 4). Where Coupling A costs survival most is not located: its grid's survival contrasts rest on two seeds per armCA-7, with no reproducibility floor (§5).

4.3 Coupling B

In the Coupling-B grid with masking on, smoke masks 0.068 %, 0.249 % and 1.559 %DX-11 of crop cells over all alive agent-steps at Low, Medium and High, and 118, 22 and 16 timesCB-10 that share at moments of danger, when a burning cell is in the crop (Figure 5(a)). At High, survival is lower with masking on than off in 3 of 3CB-5 replicate contrasts: −6.87, −10.66 and −4.70 pointsDX-13. Each rests on one run per arm, two share a seed, and none has an interval, so this cost is descriptive and ungraded. The band in Figure 5(b), a High survival floor of 6.21 pointsCB-7 from another artifact, is a reference scale (cross-artifact): a spread from other runs, shown for scale only.

At Medium, the test severity, Coupling B's own survival effect was −0.03 pointsCB-9, judged within noise against a three-seedCB-9 spread of 0.72 pointsCB-9 rather than a measured floor.

hidden commentFIGURE 5: block carried from paper/v2/sections/06_coupling_b.md:74 (include and alt text as v2 had them); caption replaced

(a) Masked share of the crop by severity, over all alive agent-steps and at moments of danger, in the Coupling-B grid (labelled “M4.4” on the figure). (b) The three High survival contrasts, then the first twoDX-40-seed estimate; the hatched band, labelled floor, is a reference scale (cross-artifact).

4.4 Communication denial

In a denial study at Medium (fourCOM-4 seeds), opening the links (δ = 0 against δ = 1) changed completion by +0.38 points and survival by −1.43 pointsCOM-4. The acceptance thresholds, 7.99 and 2.60 pointsCOM-4, were twiceV4-2 the reproducibility floor on that study's GPU (§5), and an effect as large as a threshold would clear it only about halfV4-1 the time. OWNER-PENDING:V3Q14We call communication denial "inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale" in this sense only: no effect as large as those thresholds was detected.

The label thus rests on coarse thresholds, and the design applied it asymmetrically across the stressors. Every stressor counted as behaviourally active unless calibration had found it inert, so Coupling A at High (§4.2) and Coupling B at Low and Medium (§4.3) count as active although each is weak there; only δ counts as inert. The composition the worked example tests is therefore, in effect, Coupling A with Coupling B (§6.2). OWNER-PENDING:V3Q4Remark 2′ (Appendix A) refines Pynadath & Tambe[29], who show that free communication lowers the complexity of team decision problems when the world is collectively observable. In a two-agent toy, it finds that communication has no marginal value when the agents are interchangeable, expendable and at least as numerous as the hypotheses, and regains value when any of these fails.

### 4.1 Severity, placed by a measured threshold

**Definition 4 (Severity regimes).** For an arena of side $L$, let $\xi(\beta)$ be the typical extent of a fire cluster.

- *Low*, subcritical, $\xi(\beta) \ll L$: fires stay local.
- *Medium*, near-critical, $\xi(\beta) \sim L$: one ignition can cascade to the scale of the arena.
- *High*, supercritical: the fire front moves about as fast as the agents.

The calibration ran the fire alone on all-fuel grids (Figure 3), measuring the spanning probability: the chance that one centre ignition reaches the boundary. Three estimators shipped with the calibration data place the critical point between 0.480 and 0.513<!-- N:DX-204 -->, with consensus mean 0.499<!-- N:DX-204 -->; the value fixed for the experiment, 0.500 ± 0.005<!-- N:CAL-1 -->, comes from logistic fits that no committed estimator reproduces. <!-- OWNER-PENDING:v2D2 (carried v2 question D2: Corollary 1(4) stated at the Garet and Marchand level; DISPOSITIONS §5 gives it no V3Q number) -->On the infinite square lattice, the idealized kernel's critical point is exactly ½ (Kesten, 1980)<d-cite key="kesten1980"></d-cite>, and above it a fire that spreads indefinitely burns a region growing linearly towards a deterministic convex shape (Garet & Marchand, 2004<d-cite key="garet2004chemical"></d-cite>; Corollary 1, Appendix A). ### 4.2 Coupling A

In the runs of the Coupling-B calibration grid with Coupling B on (500-update<!-- N:DX-40 --> policies at each severity), collapse-seeded ignitions per episode fall from 4.05 at Low and 3.41 at Medium to 0.84 at High<!-- N:CA-4 -->. This 4.83-fold<!-- N:CA-4 --> fall (from unrounded counts) is fuel exhaustion (a seed lights only unburnt fuel), first seen under random policies at about 5.7-fold<!-- N:DX-9 -->. The Coupling-A calibration grid (other policies, Coupling A off and on) replicates it at 4.92-fold<!-- N:CA-5 -->. In all nine<!-- N:CA-6 --> measured cells, the share of seeded ignitions inside an alive agent's crop stays between 0.164 and 0.203<!-- N:CA-6 --> (Figure 4). Where Coupling A costs survival most is not located: its grid's survival contrasts rest on two seeds per arm<!-- N:CA-7 -->, with no reproducibility floor (§5).

### 4.3 Coupling B

In the Coupling-B grid with masking on, smoke masks 0.068 %, 0.249 % and 1.559 %<!-- N:DX-11 --> of crop cells over all alive agent-steps at Low, Medium and High, and 118, 22 and 16 times<!-- N:CB-10 --> that share at moments of danger, when a burning cell is in the crop (Figure 5(a)). At High, survival is lower with masking on than off in 3 of 3<!-- N:CB-5 --> replicate contrasts: −6.87, −10.66 and −4.70 points<!-- N:DX-13 -->. Each rests on one run per arm, two share a seed, and none has an interval, so this cost is descriptive and ungraded. The band in Figure 5(b), a High survival floor of 6.21 points<!-- N:CB-7 --> from another artifact, is a reference scale (cross-artifact): a spread from other runs, shown for scale only.

At Medium, the test severity, Coupling B's own survival effect was −0.03 points<!-- N:CB-9 -->, judged within noise against a three-seed<!-- N:CB-9 --> spread of 0.72 points<!-- N:CB-9 --> rather than a measured floor.

### 4.4 Communication denial

In a denial study at Medium (four<!-- N:COM-4 --> seeds), opening the links (δ = 0 against δ = 1) changed completion by +0.38 points and survival by −1.43 points<!-- N:COM-4 -->. The acceptance thresholds, 7.99 and 2.60 points<!-- N:COM-4 -->, were twice<!-- N:V4-2 --> the reproducibility floor on that study's GPU (§5), and an effect as large as a threshold would clear it only about half<!-- N:V4-1 --> the time. <!-- OWNER-PENDING:V3Q14 -->We call communication denial "inert under a frozen random-projection encoder at this scale" in this sense only: no effect as large as those thresholds was detected.

The label thus rests on coarse thresholds, and the design applied it asymmetrically across the stressors. Every stressor counted as behaviourally active unless calibration had found it inert, so Coupling A at High (§4.2) and Coupling B at Low and Medium (§4.3) count as active although each is weak there; only δ counts as inert. The composition the worked example tests is therefore, in effect, Coupling A with Coupling B (§6.2). <!-- OWNER-PENDING:V3Q4 -->Remark 2′ (Appendix A) refines Pynadath & Tambe<d-cite key="pynadath2002"></d-cite>, who show that free communication lowers the complexity of team decision problems when the world is collectively observable. In a two-agent toy, it finds that communication has no marginal value when the agents are interchangeable, expendable and at least as numerous as the hypotheses, and regains value when any of these fails.

5 Measurement protocol

Exhibits go to Appendix C. WRITER: W3. BUDGET: 270 prose words; at most two displays. FROM: P1 §4.2 §5 and P-F; P3 §1.1 E7 (NARROW and unpositioned) and §2.2; P2 §3 A2. ORDER: - a bar comes with a floor measured on the artifact being graded, per metric and per GPU (METH-1, METH-2, HW-1; one sentence of exhibit at most, details Appendix C) - design power is the 80 %-power detection limit at the family-corrected α (METH-7; one display); define "detection limit" and "exclusion bound" here (BRIEF §5.2) - a contrast is graded on the contrast's standard error (one display, the unequal-k form §6 uses) - the floor is a check that an effect exceeds rerun noise, not the test variance (DX-21) - recorded is not graded: a channel becomes evidence only with a registered family and a floor; differenced tests cannot see effects present in both arms (say "cannot see", not "blind to") - a floor from four runs is itself uncertain by about ±40 % (DX-303; HOME) ROWS: METH-1, METH-2, METH-3, METH-4, METH-7, HW-1, DX-21, DX-303. CITATIONS: none by default (no RL evaluation-methodology work is verified in RWV; V3Q17). REGISTERED STRINGS: "recorded, not graded" (substance). CAVEATS HOMED HERE (state each in full, once; the detection pattern, from checks/caveats.yaml, must match your home sentence): C36 four_run_floor_uncertainty: A floor from four runs is itself uncertain by about ±40 %. Pattern: ±\s*40\s*% EVERY OTHER CAVEAT in checks/caveats.yaml: pointer only ("§6.3", "Appendix D"), never restated. C26: any sentence about the interaction check carries "post hoc". NOT HERE: the four bars that broke the rule, the 46-seed story, the refactor story (Appendix C, one line each); "every acceptance bar is graded against a floor" (name which have one); study codes; "instrument", "hurdle", "beat", "floor basis", "re-floor", "card" (banned); "blind to" (the check's list owns that phrase, C25). V2 MATERIAL: paper/v2/sections/08_methodology.md (§8.1 to §8.5; §8.6's shortfalls go to §7 or Appendix C). DEPENDENCIES: §6 uses these rules; Appendix C (W3) holds every floor.

GPU training is not bitwise reproducible. A reproducibility floor is the standard deviation of a metric over identical reruns of one artifact (the same code, configuration and seed) on one GPU. Under this protocol, an acceptance threshold, or bar, needs a floor measured on the artifact it grades, for that metric and GPU, or the analysis is declared underpowered. Not every comparison has a floor (Appendix C).

A design-stage power statement is a detection limit: the effect a test detects with 80 %METH-7 power at its family-corrected level \(\alpha\), computed on the contrast's standard error,

\[\mathrm{MDE}_{80} = \big(z_\alpha + z_{0.8}\big)\,\mathrm{SE}, \qquad \mathrm{SE} = \sqrt{\frac{s_A^2}{k_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{k_B}},\]

where \(z\) are standard normal quantiles and \(s_A\) and \(s_B\) are the arms' seed standard deviations over \(k_A\) and \(k_B\) seeds. A bare \(2\sigma\sqrt{2/k}\) threshold detects an effect of its own size only about halfV4-1 the time, so it only grades an observed effect against a floor, after the fact and labelled. When a confidence interval contains zero, each end is an exclusion bound: at the interval's level, the data exclude a gain above the upper end and a loss below the lower end, and the larger absolute end bounds the effect's size. The registration also calls its detection limit X an exclusion bound (§6.3).

The floor is not the test's variance: arm means also vary by seed, so contrasts are graded on seed dispersion, and the floor only checks that an effect exceeds rerun noise. A logged diagnostic becomes evidence only inside a registered test family, with a floor on its artifact; until then it is recorded, not graded. A difference between arms cannot show an effect present in both. From four identical runs, a floor is itself uncertain by about ±40 %DX-303.

GPU training is not bitwise reproducible. A reproducibility floor is the standard deviation of a metric over identical reruns of one artifact (the same code, configuration and seed) on one GPU. Under this protocol, an acceptance threshold, or bar, needs a floor measured on the artifact it grades, for that metric and GPU, or the analysis is declared underpowered. Not every comparison has a floor (Appendix C).

A design-stage power statement is a detection limit: the effect a test detects with 80 %<!-- N:METH-7 --> power at its family-corrected level $\alpha$, computed on the contrast's standard error,

$$
\mathrm{MDE}_{80} = \big(z_\alpha + z_{0.8}\big)\,\mathrm{SE}, \qquad \mathrm{SE} = \sqrt{\frac{s_A^2}{k_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{k_B}},
$$

where $z$ are standard normal quantiles and $s_A$ and $s_B$ are the arms' seed standard deviations over $k_A$ and $k_B$ seeds. A bare $2\sigma\sqrt{2/k}$ threshold detects an effect of its own size only about half<!-- N:V4-1 --> the time, so it only grades an observed effect against a floor, after the fact and labelled. The registration also calls its detection limit X an exclusion bound (§6.3).

The floor is not the test's variance: arm means also vary by seed, so contrasts are graded on seed dispersion, and the floor only checks that an effect exceeds rerun noise. A logged diagnostic becomes evidence only inside a registered test family, with a floor on its artifact; until then it is recorded, not graded. A difference between arms cannot show an effect present in both. From four identical runs, a floor is itself uncertain by about ±40 %<!-- N:DX-303 -->.

6.1 What was registered, and what changed

Before the main training runs, we pre-registered the test, its outcomes, a framing for each, and a falsifier.

hidden commentOWNER-PENDING:wording of the framing-change disclosure (V3-F5 is ruled; the words are the owner's to approve)

After the result was known, the paper's emphasis moved from the test to the environment. The registered framings remain the record of the original plan (Appendix D).

The test was sited where composition is weak. At the test severity, Coupling B's survival effect in calibration was within a three-seedCB-9 spread, not a measured floor (§4.3), and communication denial had no detectable effect (§4.4), so the test point may have offered little composition. The registration stated the Coupling-B half in advance, for every outcome: that both couplings meet their fixed-value criteria at Medium "is not the same as both producing behavioural effects there". A post hoc interaction check measures this (§6.4).

6.2 Design

The isolated arm (ISO) trains on configurations with one stressor on at a time and the joint arm (JOINT) with every stressor on, both at β = 0.43CAL-3 and 0.70CAL-3 with one shared-parameter IPPO policy per seedENV-11 [7]. Both are evaluated at θ*, the held-out test configuration: every stressor on at β = 0.49CAL-3. Architecture, hyperparameters, evaluation draw and compute (1000DES-13 updates) are matched: Γ is read at matched compute, with no claim about convergence. The gap Γ, JOINT's mean minus ISO's at θ*, is tested on two co-primaries, completion and survival, Šidák-corrected for m = 2DES-13: the confirmatory family. We ran 72DES-3 seeds per arm, above the registered cap of 60DES-1, a recorded deviation made before unblinding. Every interval is conditional on the one registered evaluation draw.

Let \(\mu_A\) and \(\mu_B\) be the per-coupling frequencies, the shares of episodes with each coupling on, and \(p\) the co-occurrence, the share with both. The active share is the share with at least one behaviourally active stressor; the fire-only share \(\nu\) is the rest. JOINT sees each coupling three times as often as ISODES-7, so Γ mixes frequency with co-occurrence. With communication denial inert (§4.4), the active share equals \(\mu_A + \mu_B - p\)X4-1: 2/3 for ISO and 1 for JOINTX4-1DES-14 (Figure 6). No contrast in this design moves co-occurrence alone.

hidden commentOWNER-PENDING:A1(ii) attribution of the review that found the bias (default "a review of the registered design")

One design error inflates Γ. Communication denial had been found inert and so was left out of the secondary sweeps, yet ISO kept two δ-only components of sixDES-16: a thirdDES-8 of ISO's training episodes are fire-only, and the design did not count them. A review of the registered design found the bias before the main runs; it was registered with its direction and a correcting arm during them, before unblindingDES-15. That arm, ISO-4, is ISO without its δ-only components (active share 1X4-1DES-14), a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family with 20DES-15 seeds. Γ₄ = JOINT − ISO-4 is the corrected gap; B̂ = ISO-4 − ISO.

ISO's single-coupling components and all of ISO-4 train with links intact (δ = 0X4-2), while JOINT trains and θ* tests at δ = 1X4-2. This train/test mismatch is harmless only if δ is inert; the post hoc interaction check (§6.4) holds δ at 1 and cannot address it.

hidden commentOWNER-PENDING:V3Q21 bias (b) label kept (default); V3Q22 registered antecedent not mentioned in the main text (default)

Bias (b), the registered caveat on differential completion drift in the last 100 updatesDES-10, reverses sign on the grid GPU (the main runs' GPU), so no direction is claimed (Appendix D).

  - 6.2 (a) arms: ISO, ISO-4 (sensitivity arm, outside the family, 20 seeds), JOINT; training severities β 0.43 and 0.70, test at the held-out θ* with every stressor on at β 0.49 (CAL-3); matched architecture, hyperparameters, evaluation draw and compute, at matched compute, never convergence (DES-13; HOME); one shared-parameter IPPO policy per seed (ENV-11); Def. 8 (Γ) in words; co-primaries completion and survival, Šidák m = 2 (DES-13); 72 seeds per arm, above the registered cap of 60, a recorded deviation made before unblinding (DES-3, DES-1; HOME, one clause; history Appendix D); every interval conditional on the one registered evaluation draw (DES-13; HOME). Figure 6.
  - 6.2 (b) the shares: per-coupling frequency μ, co-occurrence p, active share, fire-only share (define, BRIEF §5.2); JOINT sees each coupling three times as often as ISO (DES-7; HOME); the identity active share = μ_A + μ_B − p with each arm's values (X4-1; HOME): so no contrast in this design moves co-occurrence alone.
  - 6.2 (c) the design error (HOME; BRIEF §7.1, no dates): δ was already found inert; the design left it out of the secondary sweeps for that reason yet kept two δ-only components of six in ISO, so a third of ISO's episodes are fire-only (DES-8); a review of the registered design identified the bias before the grid; it was registered with its direction and the correcting arm ISO-4 before unblinding, while the grid ran (DES-15).
  - 6.2 (d) the δ train/test mismatch (HOME; use the word "mismatch"): ISO's Coupling-A-only and Coupling-B-only components and all of ISO-4 train with links intact (δ = 0), JOINT with δ = 1, and θ* has δ = 1 (X4-2); harmless only if δ is inert, the premise in question; the interaction check holds δ at 1 and cannot address it.
  - 6.2 (e) bias (b) in one sentence with the registered string "reverses sign on the grid GPU" (DES-10; full readings Appendix D).

### 6.1 What was registered, and what changed

Before the main training runs, we pre-registered the test, its outcomes, a framing for each, and a falsifier.

<!-- OWNER-PENDING:wording of the framing-change disclosure (V3-F5 is ruled; the words are the owner's to approve) -->
After the result was known, the paper's emphasis moved from the test to the environment. The registered framings remain the record of the original plan (Appendix D).

The test was sited where composition is weak. At the test severity, Coupling B's survival effect in calibration was within a three-seed<!-- N:CB-9 --> spread, not a measured floor (§4.3), and communication denial had no detectable effect (§4.4), so the test point may have offered little composition. The registration stated the Coupling-B half in advance, for every outcome: that both couplings meet their fixed-value criteria at Medium "is not the same as both producing behavioural effects there". A post hoc interaction check measures this (§6.4).

### 6.2 Design

The isolated arm (ISO) trains on configurations with one stressor on at a time and the joint arm (JOINT) with every stressor on, both at β = 0.43<!-- N:CAL-3 --> and 0.70<!-- N:CAL-3 --> with one shared-parameter IPPO policy per seed<!-- N:ENV-11 --> <d-cite key="dewitt2020ippo"></d-cite>. Both are evaluated at θ\*, the held-out test configuration: every stressor on at β = 0.49<!-- N:CAL-3 -->. Architecture, hyperparameters, evaluation draw and compute (1000<!-- N:DES-13 --> updates) are matched: Γ is read at matched compute, with no claim about convergence. The gap Γ, JOINT's mean minus ISO's at θ\*, is tested on two co-primaries, completion and survival, Šidák-corrected for m = 2<!-- N:DES-13 -->: the confirmatory family. We ran 72<!-- N:DES-3 --> seeds per arm, above the registered cap of 60<!-- N:DES-1 -->, a recorded deviation made before unblinding. Every interval is conditional on the one registered evaluation draw.

Let $\mu_A$ and $\mu_B$ be the per-coupling frequencies, the shares of episodes with each coupling on, and $p$ the co-occurrence, the share with both. The active share is the share with at least one behaviourally active stressor; the fire-only share $\nu$ is the rest. JOINT sees each coupling three times as often as ISO<!-- N:DES-7 -->, so Γ mixes frequency with co-occurrence. With communication denial inert (§4.4), the active share equals $\mu_A + \mu_B - p$<!-- N:X4-1 -->: 2/3 for ISO and 1 for JOINT<!-- N:X4-1 --><!-- N:DES-14 --> (Figure 6). No contrast in this design moves co-occurrence alone.

<!-- OWNER-PENDING:A1(ii) attribution of the review that found the bias (default "a review of the registered design") -->
One design error inflates Γ. Communication denial had been found inert and so was left out of the secondary sweeps, yet ISO kept two δ-only components of six<!-- N:DES-16 -->: a third<!-- N:DES-8 --> of ISO's training episodes are fire-only, and the design did not count them. A review of the registered design found the bias before the main runs; it was registered with its direction and a correcting arm during them, before unblinding<!-- N:DES-15 -->. That arm, ISO-4, is ISO without its δ-only components (active share 1<!-- N:X4-1 --><!-- N:DES-14 -->), a sensitivity arm outside the confirmatory family with 20<!-- N:DES-15 --> seeds. Γ₄ = JOINT − ISO-4 is the corrected gap; B̂ = ISO-4 − ISO.

ISO's single-coupling components and all of ISO-4 train with links intact (δ = 0<!-- N:X4-2 -->), while JOINT trains and θ\* tests at δ = 1<!-- N:X4-2 -->. This train/test mismatch is harmless only if δ is inert; the post hoc interaction check (§6.4) holds δ at 1 and cannot address it.

<!-- OWNER-PENDING:V3Q21 bias (b) label kept (default); V3Q22 registered antecedent not mentioned in the main text (default) -->
Bias (b), the registered caveat on differential completion drift in the last 100 updates<!-- N:DES-10 -->, reverses sign on the grid GPU (the main runs' GPU), so no direction is claimed (Appendix D).

<!-- FIGURE 6: block carried from paper/v2/sections/01_introduction.md:29 (markup, iframe height and alt text as v2 had them); caption replaced; the writer checks the alt text -->

Every secondary analysis, the plane decomposition included, is non-verdict-bearing, as registered: its 95 % intervals are not family-corrected, and its specification was ratified after Γ was seen and before the sweep was readDX-413. A sweep holds each coupling's frequency at 0.5DES-9 while \(p\) moves from 0 to 0.5DX-28, and threeDX-28 identification arms at frequency 0.4DES-9 make a plane in co-occurrence and fire-only share estimable (Appendix F).

On survival, the plane's co-occurrence slope \(\gamma_p\) is +3.26 points per unit \(p\) [+1.37, +5.02], and its fire-only-share slope \(\gamma_n\) is −2.37 points per unit \(\nu\) [−4.19, −0.57]SEC-1 (Figure 7, which prints \(\mu\) as m and \(\nu\) as n). By §6.2's identity, the plane separates co-occurrence from the active share only; per-coupling frequency moves with each slope. The fire-only-share slope predicts ISO-4 − ISO at +0.79 pointsSEC-3, inside B̂'s interval, though that move also changes frequency. The co-occurrence slope, fitted on \(p\) up to 0.5DX-28, predicts JOINT − ISO-4 at +3.26 pointsSEC-4, far above the measured Γ₄ (Table 3): it does not extrapolate to JOINT.

hidden commentFIGURE 7: block carried from paper/v2/sections/09_compositional_test.md:114 (markup, iframe height and alt text as v2 had them); caption replaced; the writer checks the alt text

On survival, Γ is +0.96 points, Šidák-corrected 95 % CI [+0.47, +1.44]RES-2: REJECT (Table 3; Figure 8 prints rates). On completion it is −1.73 points [−4.12, +0.66]RES-1, null: no detectable change, and the result excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66 pointsRES-1. The design's completion detection limit, the registration's X, is 3.29 pointsRES-1; realized power at the 3-point effect that motivated the design was 71.6 %DES-6. The registered falsifier passesRES-5 (Appendix D).

The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. Γ₄, the corrected gap, is −0.03 points on survival [−0.68, +0.63]RES-6, null, and the gain is not called a composition effect. On completion both gaps are null, so Γ₄ is a second exclusion bound, the registration's term for its detection limit, X₄ = 4.88 pointsRES-6. Γ₄ excludes only a large composition effect: its upper bound, +0.63RES-6, lies above Γ's lower bound, +0.47RES-2, and its survival detection limit is 0.90 pointsX4-4. JOINT still sees each coupling twice as often as ISO-4DES-14, and always combined. Γ₄ compares runs on two GPUs; its same-GPU replicate Γ₄′, JOINT minus the \(p = 0\) sweep arm (ISO-4's mixture on JOINT's GPU), agrees on both co-primaries but was proposed after Γ₄ was seenRES-8HW-4DES-15. B̂ is reported without a test, as registeredRES-7. Γ = Γ₄ + B̂ holds by construction for arm means, so B̂ is not a second finding. The registered headline for this outcome no longer holds as worded (Appendix D).

Γ(t), the gap at earlier checkpoints, is sign unstable over the final half on both co-primariesRES-11. On survival, its Šidák interval excludes zero only at updates 900, 950 and 1000RES-12. Completion is negative at every checkpoint from update 700RES-13 and leans negative, unresolved (Appendix E).

In agents, the survival gain is about a tenth of an agent per episode (0.11 of 12X4-3ENV-6), detectable only in the last fifth of trainingRES-12. The paper does not rest on it.

### 6.3 Results

Every secondary analysis, the plane decomposition included, is non-verdict-bearing, as registered: its 95 % intervals are not family-corrected, and its specification was ratified after Γ was seen and before the sweep was read<!-- N:DX-413 -->. A sweep holds each coupling's frequency at 0.5<!-- N:DES-9 --> while $p$ moves from 0 to 0.5<!-- N:DX-28 -->, and three<!-- N:DX-28 --> identification arms at frequency 0.4<!-- N:DES-9 --> make a plane in co-occurrence and fire-only share estimable (Appendix F).

On survival, the plane's co-occurrence slope $\gamma_p$ is +3.26 points per unit $p$ [+1.37, +5.02], and its fire-only-share slope $\gamma_n$ is −2.37 points per unit $\nu$ [−4.19, −0.57]<!-- N:SEC-1 --> (Figure 7, which prints $\mu$ as m and $\nu$ as n). By §6.2's identity, the plane separates co-occurrence from the active share only; per-coupling frequency moves with each slope. The fire-only-share slope predicts ISO-4 − ISO at +0.79 points<!-- N:SEC-3 -->, inside B̂'s interval, though that move also changes frequency. The co-occurrence slope, fitted on $p$ up to 0.5<!-- N:DX-28 -->, predicts JOINT − ISO-4 at +3.26 points<!-- N:SEC-4 -->, far above the measured Γ₄ (Table 3): it does not extrapolate to JOINT.

<!-- FIGURE 7: block carried from paper/v2/sections/09_compositional_test.md:114 (markup, iframe height and alt text as v2 had them); caption replaced; the writer checks the alt text -->

On survival, Γ is +0.96 points, Šidák-corrected 95 % CI [+0.47, +1.44]<!-- N:RES-2 -->: REJECT (Table 3; Figure 8 prints rates). On completion it is −1.73 points [−4.12, +0.66]<!-- N:RES-1 -->, null: no detectable change, and the result excludes a joint-training gain larger than 0.66 points<!-- N:RES-1 -->. The design's completion detection limit, the registration's X, is 3.29 points<!-- N:RES-1 -->; realized power at the 3-point effect that motivated the design was 71.6 %<!-- N:DES-6 -->. The registered falsifier passes<!-- N:RES-5 --> (Appendix D).

The survival gain does not survive the inert-share correction. Γ₄, the corrected gap, is −0.03 points on survival [−0.68, +0.63]<!-- N:RES-6 -->, null, and the gain is not called a composition effect. On completion both gaps are null, so Γ₄ is a second exclusion bound, the registration's term for its detection limit, X₄ = 4.88 points<!-- N:RES-6 -->. Γ₄ excludes only a large composition effect: its upper bound, +0.63<!-- N:RES-6 -->, lies above Γ's lower bound, +0.47<!-- N:RES-2 -->, and its survival detection limit is 0.90 points<!-- N:X4-4 -->. JOINT still sees each coupling twice as often as ISO-4<!-- N:DES-14 -->, and always combined. Γ₄ compares runs on two GPUs; its same-GPU replicate Γ₄′, JOINT minus the $p = 0$ sweep arm (ISO-4's mixture on JOINT's GPU), agrees on both co-primaries but was proposed after Γ₄ was seen<!-- N:RES-8 --><!-- N:HW-4 --><!-- N:DES-15 -->. B̂ is reported without a test, as registered<!-- N:RES-7 -->. Γ = Γ₄ + B̂ holds by construction for arm means, so B̂ is not a second finding. The registered headline for this outcome no longer holds as worded (Appendix D).

Γ(t), the gap at earlier checkpoints, is sign unstable over the final half on both co-primaries<!-- N:RES-11 -->. On survival, its Šidák interval excludes zero only at updates 900, 950 and 1000<!-- N:RES-12 -->. Completion is negative at every checkpoint from update 700<!-- N:RES-13 --> and leans negative, unresolved (Appendix E).

In agents, the survival gain is about a tenth of an agent per episode (0.11 of 12<!-- N:X4-3 --><!-- N:ENV-6 -->), detectable only in the last fifth of training<!-- N:RES-12 -->. The paper does not rest on it.

To measure what composition θ* offered (§6.1), we added a post hoc, evaluation-only check, planned before it ran and outside the confirmatory family (Appendix G). The final checkpoint of every ISO, JOINT and ISO-4 run is evaluated at θ* in four conditions, neither coupling, Coupling A only, Coupling B only and both, on the registered evaluation draw with common random numbers (§3.4). Per policy and co-primary, the post hoc interaction contrast is

\[I = J(\text{both}) - J(\text{A only}) - J(\text{B only}) + J(\text{neither}).\]

Higher \(J\) is better, so \(I = 0\) means that the couplings' effects add, and \(I < 0\) that together they cost more than their separate costs summed. \(I\) is estimated per arm and pooled, with Šidák-corrected intervals over eightIC-4 tests and a measured evaluation self-floor (Appendix G).

hidden commentHOLE:INTERACTION
hidden commentfills: (b) the result paragraph, pooled pair first, per PLAN §7
hidden commentHOLE:INTERACTION
hidden commentfills: Figure 9 block: the interaction figure (iframe and print companion), built after step 0.5

Besides being post hoc, the check is blind to several things. Holding policies fixed, it cannot say whether training on co-occurrence helps, which Γ tests. It covers θ* only, tests additivity only on the scale of rates, where a ceiling can create or hide an interaction, holds δ at 1COM-6, tests no difference between arms, and is conditional on the one evaluation draw. The arms also met the four conditions differently in training (Appendix G), so each arm's \(I\) also reflects its policies' familiarity with each condition, which pooling averages but does not remove.

To measure what composition θ\* offered (§6.1), we added a post hoc, evaluation-only check, planned before it ran and outside the confirmatory family (Appendix G). The final checkpoint of every ISO, JOINT and ISO-4 run is evaluated at θ\* in four conditions, neither coupling, Coupling A only, Coupling B only and both, on the registered evaluation draw with common random numbers (§3.4). Per policy and co-primary, the post hoc interaction contrast is

$$
I = J(\text{both}) - J(\text{A only}) - J(\text{B only}) + J(\text{neither}).
$$

Higher $J$ is better, so $I = 0$ means that the couplings' effects add, and $I < 0$ that together they cost more than their separate costs summed. $I$ is estimated per arm and pooled, with Šidák-corrected intervals over eight<!-- N:IC-4 --> tests and a measured evaluation self-floor (Appendix G).

Besides being post hoc, the check is blind to several things. Holding policies fixed, it cannot say whether training on co-occurrence helps, which Γ tests. It covers θ\* only, tests additivity only on the scale of rates, where a ceiling can create or hide an interaction, holds δ at 1<!-- N:COM-6 -->, tests no difference between arms, and is conditional on the one evaluation draw. 6.5 What the example teaches

STUB-SPEC (stripped at staging)STUB-SPEC JOB: The lesson (V3-F4; PROF 3; BLIND W2): by the identity, matching the active share alone cannot isolate co-occurrence, and no design in this study isolates it; the check-dependent half of the lesson; one labelled advice sentence; future work. WRITER: W5. BUDGET: 150 prose words. FROM: P1 §5.2 and §3.3; P3 §3.1 point (3); V3-F4; PAPER V3 item 4. ORDER: - (a) the identity half (write now; HOME of "cannot isolate co-occurrence"): by the identity of §6.2 (point, do not restate it), matching the active share moves per-coupling frequency, so it cannot isolate co-occurrence; the three designs side by side: the sweep fixes frequency and moves the fire-only share (DES-9, SEC-5); the correction fixes the active share and moves frequency (point to §6.3, do not say "twice as often"); the plane fixes the fire-only share and moves frequency with p. None isolates co-occurrence. - (b) the check-dependent half (HOLE): one or two sentences from the matching row of P1 §3.3, labelled post hoc. - (c) advice, if any: one sentence of its own, labelled "We suggest, from this one environment, …"; never after therefore, so or thus; not a contribution. - (d) future work: ISO-4 trained for 2000 updates, which would match each coupling's exposure count to JOINT's but gives up matched compute (PAPER V3 item 4; no run authorized); varying coupling strength instead of frequency. A third item (a test point where both couplings are active) only if the owner adds it (V3Q20). ROWS: DES-9, DES-14 (pointer), SEC-5, X4-1 (pointer), IC-* (for the HOLE). REGISTERED STRINGS: none required; "non-verdict-bearing" is not repeated here. CAVEATS HOMED HERE (state each in full, once; the detection pattern, from checks/caveats.yaml, must match your home sentence): C27 cannot_isolate_co_occurrence: Matching the active share alone cannot isolate co-occurrence; no design here isolates it (the lesson, never after "therefore"). Pattern: (?i)\bisolat\w* co-occurrence\b SHORT FORMS ALLOWED HERE (at most one sentence each; the full statement is at the home): C08 active_share_identity (home 06_example_design.md) C11 matched_compute_never_convergence (home 06_example_design.md) EVERY OTHER CAVEAT in checks/caveats.yaml: pointer only ("§6.3", "Appendix D"), never restated. C26: any sentence about the interaction check carries "post hoc". HOLES: - (b) the check-dependent lesson - (c) the advice sentence's content, which depends on (b) NOT HERE: "match the active share" as advice (by the identity it un-matches frequency); "the plane separates co-occurrence from frequency"; "therefore"; "twice as often" (home §6.3); the identity restated in full (home §6.2); any claim about Low, High or training effects from the check. V2 MATERIAL: paper/v2/sections/09_compositional_test.md §9.3 (replaced, not reused). DEPENDENCIES: §6.2 (identity), §6.3 (Γ₄'s limits), §6.4 (the check); the abstract's lesson (W1) must match (b) and (c).

By the identity of §6.2, holding the active share fixed while co-occurrence changes forces per-coupling frequency to change with it, so matching the active share alone cannot isolate co-occurrence. None of the study's designs isolates it. The sweep holds each coupling's frequency fixed and moves the fire-only share with co-occurrence. The inert-share correction holds the active share fixed and moves frequency along with co-occurrence (§6.3). The plane's co-occurrence slope holds the fire-only share fixed and moves frequency with co-occurrence.

hidden commentHOLE:INTERACTION
hidden commentfills: (b) the check-dependent half of the lesson, labelled post hoc; (c) the labelled advice sentence

Two experiments would go further. ISO-4 trained for 2000X5-3 updates would meet each coupling as often as JOINT does in the registered budget, at the cost of matched compute, leaving co-occurrence as the main remaining difference. Varying the couplings' strength, with frequency, co-occurrence and the active share fixed, would test whether composition appears where both couplings are strong (§6.1).

### 6.5 What the example teaches

<!-- STUB-SPEC
JOB: The lesson (V3-F4; PROF 3; BLIND W2): by the identity, matching the active share alone cannot isolate co-occurrence, and no design in this study isolates it; the check-dependent half of the lesson; one labelled advice sentence; future work.
WRITER: W5.
BUDGET: 150 prose words.
FROM: P1 §5.2 and §3.3; P3 §3.1 point (3); V3-F4; PAPER V3 item 4.
ORDER:
  - (a) the identity half (write now; HOME of "cannot isolate co-occurrence"): by the identity of §6.2 (point, do not restate it), matching the active share moves per-coupling frequency, so it cannot isolate co-occurrence; the three designs side by side: the sweep fixes frequency and moves the fire-only share (DES-9, SEC-5); the correction fixes the active share and moves frequency (point to §6.3, do not say "twice as often"); the plane fixes the fire-only share and moves frequency with p. None isolates co-occurrence.
  - (b) the check-dependent half (HOLE): one or two sentences from the matching row of P1 §3.3, labelled post hoc.

V2 MATERIAL: paper/v2/sections/09_compositional_test.md §9.3 (replaced, not reused).

By the identity of §6.2, holding the active share fixed while co-occurrence changes forces per-coupling frequency to change with it, so matching the active share alone cannot isolate co-occurrence. None of the study's designs isolates it. The sweep holds each coupling's frequency fixed and moves the fire-only share with co-occurrence. The inert-share correction holds the active share fixed and moves frequency along with co-occurrence (§6.3). The plane's co-occurrence slope holds the fire-only share fixed and moves frequency with co-occurrence.

Varying the couplings' strength, with frequency, co-occurrence and the active share fixed, would test whether composition appears where both couplings are strong (§6.1).

7 Limitations and scope

FROM: P1 §4.2 §7; P3 §2.2 and §1.4; READS RULING item 4. ORDER: - matched compute, never convergence (one line; DES-13) - no theorem says joint training wins (TF:686; HOME) - one budget; one held-out point between the training severities (pointer §6.2) - the d_p ablation's scope (ENV-2 to ENV-5: 500 updates, 3 seeds, fire only) - no heuristic baselines; the task needs no coordination and the agents share parameters (pointer §1) - no adaptive curriculum is evaluated (READS RULING item 4) - cross-GPU blocks and the δ train/test difference by pointer only (§6.3, §6.2) ROWS: DES-13, ENV-2, ENV-3, ENV-4, ENV-5, ENV-11. CAVEATS HOMED HERE (state each in full, once; the detection pattern, from checks/caveats.yaml, must match your home sentence): C38 no_theorem_joint_wins: No theorem says joint training wins. Pattern: (?i)\bno theorem\b SHORT FORMS ALLOWED HERE (at most one sentence each; the full statement is at the home): C11 matched_compute_never_convergence (home 06_example_design.md) EVERY OTHER CAVEAT in checks/caveats.yaml: pointer only ("§6.3", "Appendix D"), never restated. C26: any sentence about the interaction check carries "post hoc". NOT HERE: anything §1 already states (sited where composition is weak; the inert label; one algorithm, one map, 12 agents) beyond a pointer; the word "mismatch" (home §6.2); secondary intervals not family-corrected (home §6.3); dates; Proposition 4. V2 MATERIAL: paper/v2/sections/10_limitations.md. DEPENDENCIES: §1 (W1) carries the three up-front limitations.

§1 lists the largest limitations first, and each sits where it arises: how much composition the test configuration offered, and what the post hoc interaction check measured there (§6.1, §6.4); the coarse and unevenly applied inert label for communication denial (§4.4); the communication setting that differs between training and test for two arms (§6.2); and one learner, one map and one team size (§1).

Every comparison in the worked example is at matched compute, 1000DES-13 updates per run, and says nothing about other budgets (§6.2). No theorem in this paper says that joint training should win; the hypothesis the worked example tests is empirical. The test configuration is one held-out severity between the two training severities (§6.2). The hazard is simple, with one ignition at reset, a one-step burn and no wind or uneven fuel (§3.1). The only ablation of the death price is narrow, with three seedsENV-2, 500ENV-2 updates and the fire as the only hazard (§3.3). The task needs no coordination, the agents share one set of parameters, and no heuristic or scripted baseline is reported (§1). We compare fixed training distributions and evaluate no adaptive curriculum. Comparisons across GPUs are marked where they occur (§6.3).

CHE is a functional JAX environment: reset(key, cfg) returns observations and state, and step(key, state, actions, cfg) also returns the team reward, a done flag and an info dictionary. YAML configurations set θ, the fixed sub-parameters and any training mixture, and a test checks them against a registry of the fixed constants (Appendix B).

In the anonymized code supplement, uv run pytest -q che/tests runs the CPU tests, and uv run python -m che.train.ippo and uv run python -m che.eval.harness train and evaluate. On one RTX 5090DX-5, a 1000-update run took 518–521 s and a 512-episode evaluation 9–10 sDX-4.

The supplement holds the package, configurations, tests, registration documents, analysis scripts with the committed outputs they read, and a sha256 manifest; its README recomputes the registered analyses on a CPU. The post hoc interaction check's analysis needs version history, so it does not run there. Checkpoints ship only as hashes.

hidden commentOWNER-PENDING:V3Q16
hidden commentwhat else ships is the owner's call (RM-OWNER-1): the supplement builder ships docs/theory_foundations.md and the four lock records by default (paper/v3/supplement/README.md, rules D3, D4), while DISPOSITIONS V3Q16's default ships the hash manifest only; if they ship, add "the theory document and the records of how each fixed constant was measured" to the list above. Decision-log excerpts are off by default (V3-V1 owner decision). The supplement's size and TMLR's limit wait for rows X5-1 and X5-2 (PENDING; V3Q24, V3Q25) and are not stated.
hidden commentOWNER-PENDING:V3_V2 the registration statement's wording, in particular the commitment to a public registry next time, is the owner's to confirm

Our registration record is internal and version-controlled, so its dates are self-reported; the hash manifest shows only that the archive arrived unaltered (Appendix D). A public registry such as OSF would remove this limit; we intend to use one next time.

Evaluation reproduces closely: repeated evaluations of two checkpoints on one GPU were bit-identical and matched the main runs' ownHW-2, and across GPUs 280 of 288HW-3 paired checkpoint and metric values agreed exactly, differing by at most 2.44 × 10⁻⁴HW-3. GPU training is not, which is why reproducibility floors are measured (§5).
